Josh O’Connor Should Be James Bond

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The Challengers star would bring a new energy—and a new brand of modern masculinity—to the world’s most famous spy.

WHEN THE NAMES of the rumored final contenders to play the next James Bond were floated in the press a couple of weeks ago—seemingly Callum Turner, Jack Lowden, Josh O’Connor, and a mystery third candidate, according to Deadline—I heard a lot of the same sentiment afterward. In more or less words, a lot of people seemed to say “I love Josh O’Connor, but he shouldn’t be Bond.” When I dropped the link into a group chat full of people who aren’t inhaling movie news like I am, there was another probably common response: “I don’t know who that guy is, but he doesn’t look like Bond.”

To me, that sentiment is exactly why O’Connor is the best—and, in some ways, the only—choice to be the next James Bond.

For those not familiar, O’Connor, a 36-year-old Brit who grew up in Cheltenham, has been a working actor for more than a decade, but his star has only really begun to shine in the 2020s. He won an Emmy for playing Prince Charles in The Crown, and more recently has made splashes in movies like the thrilling and sexy sports film Challengers, Rian Johnson’s third Knives Out film Wake Up Dead Man (which, ironically, he appeared in alongside previous Bond Daniel Craig), and this summer’s Steven Spielberg blockbuster Disclosure Day.

It’s not hard to understand why some people’s minds might naturally push against the idea of O’Connor as Bond. Where 007 is typically a classically handsome, devilishly smooth gentleman who almost looks like a video game simulation—seriously, look back at Sean Connery and Pierce Brosnan for just a second—O’Connor is a bit off-kilter. His hair is wavy; his ears are prominent; his smile is asymmetrical. There’s no beating around the bush—he’s been compared more often to Linguini from Pixar’s Ratatouille than the idea most people have of what a James Bond should be.

But it’s through those eccentricities that O’Connor shines. If you take the time to watch his performances, you’ll find the constant image of someone who brings an undeniable charisma to every project, and somehow manages to mold that into something different each time. In Challengers, O’Connor is the kind of guy you want to root against—he’s sleazy, not a particularly good friend, and unrefined. But he brings the perfect bit of steamy, compelling energy to a love triangle that wouldn’t be the same without him. Co-stars Mike Faist and Zendaya are deserving of credit (as are composers Trent Reznor and Atticus Ross), but O’Connor’s charm is what makes the whole movie work.

He pulls a similar magic trick with just about every other performance. In Wake Up Dead Man, he plays a former boxer-turned-priest who once accidentally killed a man in the ring. Throughout the course of the film, O’Connor goes from someone who wrestles with guilt, shame, and intrigue to someone who can believably talk someone struggling with grief off a ledge—all while both trying to solve a murder and possibly being suspected of that same murder at the same time. In Kelly Reichardt’s The Mastermind, O’Connor plays a less-than-competent, hardly aspirational art thief who stumbles his way through charged political times. Despite this, O’Connor is so magnetic on screen that it’s just about impossible not to like him.

But it’s not just the way he elevates sleazeballs and losers into undeniably watchable men (although that doesn’t hurt). O’Connor has shown a propensity to play traditionally enchanting as well. In La Chimera, from Italian filmmaker Alice Rohrwacher, he’s cool, sleek, and morally gray in a movie that may be the blueprint for what his Bond could potentially look like. He’s got a likable way about him that’s different from some of the manly men (like Connery), sleek operators (like Brosnan), or rugged action stars (like Craig) who’ve played the role before; His charm is distinctly more modern, oozing from nothing other than being a guy who knows how to talk to people, and do it really well.

O’Connor’s quirks and oddities, may, at first glance, be what seems to make him an unfit choice to play Bond. But the key is to look at O’Connor’s unique look and gifts as a feature rather than a bug. People might say Bond needs to look this way, and sound that way, and otherwise it doesn’t work. But for anyone who feels so strongly about that, there’s good news: There are 25 other canonical James Bond movies (plus 1967’s Casino Royale and 1983’s Never Say Never Again) where he almost always looks and behaves just like that. When better than now to try something new? Daniel Craig’s blond hair and untraditional look were also questioned at first, and it’s safe to say that risk turned out pretty well in the end.

The forthcoming Bond movie has Denis Villeneuve signed on as director, and Villeneuve has pretty much never failed when it comes to casting his movies. Can you imagine anyone other than Timothée Chalamet as Paul Atreides in his Dune movies? It seems like an obvious pick now, but the 30-year-old Oscar nominee had only just begun to break out when he was cast in the role back in 2018. Villeneuve has a clear eye for talent, and so it won’t be much of a stretch to trust in whoever ultimately lands the job—but it’s hard to not be excited about a director of his caliber linking up with an actor as ambitious and high-reaching as O’Connor.

Plus, it’s worth noting that the Bond franchise isn’t quite like other mega-series out there. Since the Craig era began with 2006’s Casino Royale, Bond movies have come out on average every three or four years. That’s spread out enough for O’Connor to continue taking on fun, exciting projects even outside of the role as everyone’s favorite super spy.

The other choices aren’t bad. Callum Turner gets a bad rap after his appearance in One Night Only, but he’s shown promise in projects like Green Room and Masters of the Air, and Apple TV’s new Neuromancer looks good. Jack Lowden has played one of television’s most beloved spies for six seasons on Slow Horses—the fit would be seamless. Whoever Villeneuve picks (even if it’s the unnamed mystery actor) will be afforded a huge amount of goodwill.

But isn’t the fact that O’Connor elicits such a reaction—consistently in his on-screen roles and through the mere idea of him becoming James Bond—proof that he’s the right pick?

EVgo opens its first Tesla Superchargers, with hundreds more planned

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

EVgo has opened its first Supercharger station, and it’s in a Chicago grocery store parking lot. The site has 12 fast-charging stalls with both NACS and CCS plugs, and it’s the first of hundreds of Tesla Supercharger stalls EVgo plans to add across the US.

The 325 kW “EVgo Superchargers” are at Riverside Square & River’s Edge, at 3145 S Ashland Avenue. EVgo’s August announcement cited up to 500 kW for the Supercharger rollout.

The Chicago location features Magic Dock technology, which means both NACS and CCS vehicles can charge without an adapter. Drivers of compatible non-Tesla EVs can start a session through the Tesla app. If you drive a Tesla, the charging station shows up in the car’s navigation and Trip Planner. Other EVgo fast chargers with NACS plugs have been showing up in Tesla navigation since early September if you turn on third-party charging.

“By serving both Tesla and non-Tesla NACS drivers, we’re doubling EVgo’s addressable market and continuing to meet EV drivers where they are and where they want to charge,” said Badar Khan, EVgo CEO.

EVgo owns the Superchargers and self-brands them, and Tesla runs and maintains them. The first sites will be at Regency Centers shopping plazas with grocery stores.

EVgo is also adding NACS plugs to its regular fast-charging network, a rollout it started in 2025. The company aims to have 500 of those stalls across the US by the end of 2026, with Supercharger stalls counted separately. Over the next two years, it plans to put at least one NACS plug at every EVgo site built in 2023 or later.

Silversea unveils the southernmost hotel in the world, purpose-built for Antarctica trips

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

There’s now a new reason to travel to the bottom of the world.

Silversea on Thursday officially opened The Cormorant at 55 South, a new luxury hotel near the southern tip of South America designed to transform the experience of traveling to Antarctica.

Located in Chile's Patagonia region near Puerto Williams, the world's southernmost settlement, the 150-room property will serve as a luxury base and launch pad for Silversea's high-end fly-cruise trips to Antarctica — journeys that begin with a flight from Puerto Williams to a research station at Antarctica's King George Island.

Once there, travelers board a Silversea expedition ship to explore Antarctica.

Related: How to get to Antarctica: The pros and cons of flying vs. cruising

“Silversea has always been about going further, and for our guests heading to Antarctica, that journey now begins here at the southernmost hotel on Earth, with the kind of welcome they’ve come to expect from us,” Silversea president Bert Hernandez said during an unveiling event at the hotel.

TPG is one of just a handful of travel media outlets getting an early look at the property this week.

Until now, Silversea's fly-cruise passengers have stayed in non-Silversea hotels in Punta Arenas, Chile ahead of a charter flight to King George Island. The less-than-ideal arrangement meant guests were booked across various hotels, while the line was left scrambling for additional rooms whenever bad weather delayed or grounded flights to Antarctica.

Now, with The Cormorant at 55 South, guests can relax in modern luxury overnight — or sit out bad weather — just minutes from their charter flight. Better yet, the expedition begins well before they step aboard their ship.

While at the hotel, guests will be able to try on or swap their Silversea-provided Antarctica polar gear, complete biosecurity checks that are necessary before visiting the continent, and clean any personal equipment they plan to take ashore, as well as attend an initial Antarctica briefing — activities that usually take place on the ship.

Related: I just camped on the ice in Antarctica; here's how you can, too

They'll also get a chance to explore Patagonia’s breathtaking wilderness on guided excursions around the hotel, including kayaking, hiking, birdwatching and horseback riding. A shuttle bus will also take guests into Puerto Williams.

“The idea was that this wasn’t a stopover hotel. This is the first and last impression that we make on our guests, and so we wanted that to be memorable,” Hernandez said at a press conference ahead of an official ribbon-cutting ceremony.

Initially conceived in 2018 as a turnaround hub for Antarctica, the Cormorant at 55 South has become a destination that by Hernandez’s own admission is “more beautiful than anyone ever envisioned."

Designed to blend in with the environment, the low-slung hotel features soaring ceilings, concrete floors, natural wood, stone and earthy textiles punctuated by pops of orange, yellow and green — colours that echo the surrounding terrain.

Floor-to-ceiling glass means the view is never far away, be it forest, sea or snow-scratched mountains, stretching from the jagged peaks of Dientes de Navarino to the Andes in Argentina.

Related: The best Antarctica cruise vessels

“It was always incredibly important that the hotel reflect the environment,” Silversea senior vice president of expeditions Conrad Combrink said during unveiling events. Elements of fire and ice inform the design, alongside the darkness of the Drake Passage and the Beagle Channel, just beyond the hotel.

Being in the “land of fire”, the hotel fittingly has a massive indoor firepit, surrounded by lounges and easy chairs. An outside terrace offers more places to sit and take in the scenery.

Rooms are simply designed, with a view of mountain or sea, and pair a soothing mix of local wood, natural textiles and green upholstery. There’s a Nespresso machine and a small bar fridge with water and craft beer from a brewery in Punta Arenas.

Latitude 55, the onsite restaurant, showcases regional flavors, including excellent empanadas and a stand-out asado barbecue served on the first night of any stay, slow-cooked over an open fire in the hotel grounds.. At Bar Sur, guests can sample Chilean wine, enjoy a pisco sour tasting or try a cocktail made with calafete, a berry indigenous to Puerto Williams. The Uncharted Boutique is the place to stock up on beautifully made Chilean handicrafts or last-minute polar gear.

More than a hotel stay, The Cormorant at 55 South marks a wider shift for Silversea,which will now base all its Antarctica expeditions out of Puerto Williams. Previously only sailings that crossed the Drake Passage left from Puerto Williams.

While most Antarctic operators base their fleets out of Ushuaia, Argentina, Silversea is the only cruise line running operations out of Puerto Williams. Located further south along the Beagle Channel, Puerto Williams provides a shorter, more direct launch point into the Drake Passage.

Silversea's fly-cruise trips allow Antarctica-bound travelers to skip a ship-based voyage across the Drake Passage — the notoriously rough, 600-mile-wide waterway separating the tip of South America and Antarctica.

Guests fly from Santiago directly to Puerto Williams for an overnight stay at The Cormorant, before taking a charter flight across the Drake to King George Island in Antarctica's South Shetland Islands, where their expedition ship awaits. Upon return, guests spend another night at the hotel before flying back to Santiago.

Related: The ultimate guide to expedition cruising to Antarctica and beyond

The opening of the Cormorant at 55 South coincides with Silversea's largest Antarctic season to date. Fly-cruise itineraries now dominate the line’s offerings in Antarctica, accounting for 29 of its 40 total sailings across three expedition vessels: Silver Endeavour, Silver Wind, and Silver Cloud. The hotel will currently stay open from October through March, but there is talk it will open year-round, for travelers to southern Chile looking for a soft landing after adventures.

More than a place to stay,  The Cormorant makes the journey south feel like an experience in its own right — a fittingly rugged starting point for what lies ahead, plus unique bragging rights of having stayed at the southernmost hotel on earth.

8 Reasons a Contractor Might Give You an Outrageously High Estimate

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

If you just got a quote for a home repair that made your eyes water, there's probably a reason it's so high. A team of contractors weighs in with possible explanations.

If you just got a quote from a contractor that was way more than you thought it would be, join the club…it happens to the best of us. In fact, it’s a pretty common occurrence. Sometimes, though, the quote is so high that you can feel your mouth forming words you shouldn’t be saying. As someone who has been on both sides of such interactions, I can say that contractors don’t like them any more than you do. They know you can shop around, and they need your business. So what’s the deal with ridiculously high quotes?

I didn’t find any shortage of contractors willing to weigh in on this topic — one that is near and dear to their professional hearts. If a quote just made you hyperventilate, take a deep breath and check out some of the possible reasons why it’s so much higher than you expected.

“If an HVAC unit has a defective part that needs replacement, most of my clients will immediately ask how much the replacement part costs,” says HVAC technician Tamer Sayed. “I always tell the current market price and the misunderstanding often starts here. A few clients forget to take into account the labor and consultation fees. They assume they only had to pay for the replacement part.”

The same thing can happen with plumbing and electrical jobs. The part needed to get things working again may be small and inexpensive, and it may only account for a small portion of the quote. Sometimes the labor quote seems excessive, and that’s usually because the client doesn’t understand what’s involved with the repair.

Jake Vincett, a builder from the UK, makes this observation: “A lot of the time, the customer is comparing quotes for different work, because the cheaper one has included a surface repair whereas the more expensive one has quoted for fixing the underlying problem…It’s common that the customer doesn’t know or consider the amount of work going into a project. A new driveway isn’t just laying tarmac – it’s digging out the old one, laying the sub base, etc.”

Plumbing, electrical and HVAC expert Bob Cardwell adds: “It’s often not only one problem to fix but a bunch of small fixes. Even though it might be possible to fix the original problem without addressing the additional issues, an experienced and reliable technician will not ignore them, will try to fix them and will discuss them with the homeowner.”

Failing to provide a quote that addresses underlying issues and then delivering a sky-high bill at the end doesn’t just make the customer unhappy, says HVAC and plumbing expert Ryan Osterkamp. “It can make them feel like we don’t know what we’re doing.”

Osterkamp is used to hearing customers say: “I can go get that part for $X at Home Depot.” What they aren’t thinking about is the amount of training and expertise that made it possible for our technician to quickly and accurately diagnose the issue, have the supplies they need in their truck and be able to do the repair same day. Sure, you may save money by going with the guy on Facebook that works out of an old, unlabeled truck, but if his work doesn’t hold up, will he fix it without giving you the runaround? Is he insured? Does he offer a warranty?”

Tariffs, inflation and the economy in general are pushing prices up, and the dollar has lost a lot of its purchasing power for the average American. The same is true for contractors. “The fact of the matter is, we’re working harder now and making less,” says remodeling contractor Kevin Cullen. “In today’s economy, a lot of people are chasing the price to the bottom and shopping for the cheapest number. Unfortunately, they usually get what they pay for.”

A contractor who knows problems might creep up, such as a rotten subfloor that needs replacement during a toilet repair, makes an effort to warn customers as part of the quote. Potential problems aren’t always obvious. “There are times,” says Nika Tveretinova, who operates an HVAC business in Sacramento, “when the actual fix isn’t that complicated, but it’s the access that makes things tough—especially with equipment installed in an attic or cramped quarters. Sometimes we also find an old installation that needs a safety or code correction before we can put everything back together.”

In such situations, a higher-than-expected quote that you can reject is far better than a higher-than-expected bill you have to pay because the work is already completed.

Joshua Kurz founded NumeralQ, a home improvement pricing guide, and he cautions against comparing a bid you receive from a contractor to information you find online. “Owners often find an estimate online, and rather than waiting for a bid, they assume that number will be the actual cost. These estimates are helpful when determining a budget, but they are not interchangeable for actual bids. A 1,500 sq ft roof in one market will not necessarily cost the same in another market, and bids can even differ for neighboring houses.”

If you find a guy on Facebook who charges a low price to do a repair, there’s no guarantee that the repair will last or be up to code. When that person makes a mistake, a licensed contractor working on the same system at a later date might have to fix it, especially when inspectors are involved.

“Over a 40-year career,” says Cullen, “I’ve found many surprises: unsupported headers, no king or jack studs, undersized headers, poorly installed headers that aren’t flush and have gaps, termite damage, carpenter ant damage, water damage, mold. That’s not every job, but you never know when a homeowner bought a house in which a Rube Goldberg did the work on it.”

Sometimes a house is in such poor condition that a small repair, such as stopping a plumbing leak, can turn into a major project that involves replacing pipes, walls or subfloors. Rather than simply declining the repair, some contractors will offer a ridiculously high quote to avoid falling into a sinkhole of extra work. If the customer accepts the quote, the contractor won’t lose money by taking the job. A customer who rejects a bid can keep shopping with no harm done.

Bear Grylls Bought a ‘Derelict’ Island With ‘No Power’ and ‘Rats’ at 25 Instead of a London Apartment—Here’s How It Looks Decades Later

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Bear Grylls made an unusual decision decades ago, when he acquired a marital starter home with his wife, Shara Grylls.

“At 25, just married, we bought a small, abandoned, off-grid island from an ad in the back of a magazine,” the British adventurer, 52, wrote in a Nov. 3 Instagram caption.

Grylls revealed that the purchase of St. Tudwal’s Island West cost them around $120,000.

“There were many who thought us crazy,” he continued. “The best adventures start with nay-sayers. Thank you Shara for being brave and saying yes!”

While the comment section had users divided over the relatability of purchasing an entire island—with one commenter wondering how “celebrities get so far out of touch from reality and regular people”—there were also a handful of people who stepped in to point out that even when adjusted for inflation, the island was less costly than the average U.S. home.

The “Running Wild With Bear Grylls” star explained the decision to the Sunday Times in 2021, noting that snapping up a Welsh island seemed like a more exciting use of the couple’s money.

“In 2001, we were going to buy a London flat for $288,000, but we bought a houseboat on the Thames [now sold] and the island together, and it cost less than the apartment. So we decided on the path less trodden and an amazing adventure,” he said.

Grylls shed some light on just how dilapidated the property was 25 years ago.

“The island had a 200-year-old lighthouse and two lightkeeper’s cottages, but [the houses] were derelict with no power, no water, no roof, and rats,” he said. “I remember my first bath sitting in 3 inches of water pumped from the old tanks we’d cleaned out. There was rubble everywhere and rain coming through, but it was a great moment.”

Considering Grylls has made a career out of his ability to survive in extremely harsh conditions, it’s not entirely surprising that he was able to get through a few rough years before the island would become a more suitable dwelling.

It took a decade to get permission to build a slipway, which Grylls said they finished just a few years ago.

“It has been life-changing for us,” he said of the addition. “Previously we had to keep a boat on a mooring, row ashore, and walk a mile up the beach with all the shopping.”

Eventually, the two lightkeeper’s cottages were combined into a single home with four bedrooms. But the island is still off-grid.

“It’s 20 acres and 5 miles offshore with no mains, electricity, or running water,” Grylls told Vanity Fair in 2012. “It has a little lighthouse beside our house and is surrounded by amazing sea cliffs, seals, and dolphins. It is my favorite place on the planet!”

To travel to the destination, Grylls and his family take a “30-foot, high-speed ex-lifeboat,” which he described as a “rigid inflatable boat with twin 300-horsepower engines behind it that can tackle any sea it needs to.”

He said the house is “as tough as you get.” Thanks to “2-foot-thick old limestone walls,” it retains heat in the winter and stays cool in the summer.

“But when those gales come in, you can still feel it almost shake,” Grylls admitted. “Sea spray reaches the windows despite the 150-foot elevation.”

While St. Tudwal’s may lack some of the modern amenities guests might typically expect, the Grylls family does “sometimes have friends” visit.

“They need to be up for an adventure and prepared to get stuck there if the weather turns,” he said.

Grylls has recently made headlines for his connection to disgraced former comic Russell Brand, who was recently found guilty of one count of rape and three counts of sexual assault.

Grylls helped baptize Brand in a ceremony in the River Thames in 2024, after the allegations against the actor had come to light. Prior to the baptism, Brand appeared in a 2023 episode of Grylls’ show, in which the pair traveled across the Hebrides Islands.

“Brand’s faith seemed genuine to me at the time when I was a guest at his private baptism,” Grylls wrote in a Nov. 6 X post. “I have attended a few now. Even visiting in prisons with people who have committed terrible crimes. It’s part of our calling as men and women of faith, to love and serve anyone. It is the sick that need healing.”

Noting that “judgment is for the courts and God,” Grylls added that “it is right that justice has now been served.

“I can’t imagine how hard this time has been for the victims, the heroes of the day,” he said.

She has been writing entertainment and lifestyle content since 2016, with her work appearing in Cosmopolitan, BravoTV, NYLON, Bustle, Elite Daily, and The Zoe Report, among other publications. Hannah received a bachelor’s degree in journalism from The University of Maine. In her spare time, she loves to chat about pop culture and is a major fan of reality TV.

I Thought Multi-Tools Were a Gimmick — Then I Took One on a Camping Trip

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

If, like me, you think multi-tools are gimmicky gadgets, you might change your mind once you discover how handy they are when your toolbox isn’t within reach.

Our editors and experts handpick every product we feature. We may earn a commission from your purchases.Learn more.

I admit I scoffed when my partner first started raving about his Leatherman Wave+ multi-tool. I thought it looked like an expensive, over-elaborate gadget that was destined to gather dust in a junk drawer.

However, as a keen hiker and camper, I’ve discovered how useful it is to have pliers, knives, a saw, scissors, files and other tools packed into one compact, quality piece of kit. I’ve also watched my tech-obsessed partner regularly reach for its precision tools when tinkering with his electrical equipment.

Ahead, I’ll share how a tool I once dismissed as a gimmick earned a permanent spot in my pack, and what two experts think makes a Leatherman worth the price tag.

I had to realize that our Leatherman Wave+ isn’t meant to replace a well-stocked toolbox to understand its value. Instead, it fills the gap when dedicated tools aren’t conveniently available or really needed.

At home, I regularly use the tool’s tiny scissors to carefully prune dead leaves from our many houseplants, the knife to open packages and the small screwdriver to tighten the legs on my glasses. My partner is an IT professional who also has an impressive collection of synthesizers, and he regularly reaches for the tool to make small technical repairs and splice wires.

I’ve found the Wave+ even more useful when we’re borrowing my parents’ campervan, hiking, or camping. The bottle opener is handy for those after-adventure beers, the serrated knife slices baguettes for trailside sandwiches, the saw cuts small, dry branches for kindling, and the screwdriver has adjusted our portable stove and tightened folding chairs.

The pocket-sized multi-tool packs 18 functions into a stainless-steel form that measures just four inches when closed and weighs 8.5 ounces. Alongside the tools I’ve mentioned, it has pliers, bit drivers, a wire stripper and crimper, a ruler, an extra knife blade and file, and an admittedly slightly-tricky-to-use can opener.

While I don’t regularly use all 18 tools, and I might never use some, having the selection provides peace of mind, especially in remote places.

Daniel Kilburn, founder of Emergency Action Planning and a preparedness expert, has carried his Leatherman Surge since his military service in 2006. “It’s an expedient tool to have on hand when you know you’re going to be someplace where you won’t have access to any others,” he says.

Scott Emanuel of knives, multi-tools, and outdoor equipment retailer Heinnie Haynes is also a multi-tool convert. He appreciated having one for quick fixes while renovating his cottage, and particularly rates the needle-nose pliers, which rival standard models rather than acting as an emergency substitute.

Kilburn suggests multi-tools can be a useful starting point for someone who doesn’t own a toolbox, and at the other end of the spectrum are the tinkerers and prepared problem-solvers Emanuel sees as natural fits.

“The person who will value a Leatherman is the one who spends their after-hours in the garage building stuff that they probably never need, it’s the kind of self-reliant and prepared person who you rely on to solve a problem,” Emanuel says.

The Leatherman Wave+ currently costs $129.95, meaning it should be a well-considered purchase rather than an impulse buy.

It’s reassuring to know that you’ll be investing in a refined product designed because of a genuine practical need. Founder Tim Leatherman conceived a pliers-based multi-tool after encountering car and plumbing problems while traveling through Europe in the 1970s, and after careful development, he launched the original Pocket Survival Tool in 1983.

Today you’re paying for that established reputation and durable construction, and Leatherman backs its products with a 25-year warranty.

“They’re ridiculously well built, really well regarded, and their choice of tools is good,” Emanuel says. He describes sought-after Leathermans as the “collectible top end of the multi-tool market.”

Kilburn recalls seeing lower-priced multi-tools failing his comrades during their military career. “Some disintegrate over time because they were just cheaply made with poor-quality material [and] poor-quality steel,” he says. His Leatherman Surge, meanwhile, has lasted over 20 years. If replacing it again, he says he would choose Leatherman over his backup Gerber multi-tool because he prefers its feel and durability.

Leatherman makes a wide range of multi-tools, so it pays to consider which functions you’ll genuinely use. The Signal model adds outdoor-focused features suited to camping, including a ferrocerium rod, emergency whistle and sharpener, but omits scissors. I use scissors enough for plant pruning, first aid and gear repairs that the Wave+ remains the better fit for me.

If you’re unlikely to use most of its functions, a smaller, cheaper multi-tool could make more sense. The Wave+ is also heavier than minimalist models, and there’s a learning curve when figuring out where everything lives.

Don’t forget that a premium multi-tool only represents good value if you look after it. We remove dirt after outdoor adventures and dry and lubricate it before storing. We also avoid forcing it to tackle jobs better suited to full-size tools. I wouldn’t, for example, choose its compact saw over a dedicated full-size version for pruning substantial tree branches in my yard.

If you’re using a Wave+ in the UK, like me, be aware of the country’s knife laws. You need a “good reason” to carry it in public because its blades lock. However, Emanuel explains that the legal language is ambiguous, so you shouldn’t assume you can carry one wherever you go just because it’s part of a multi-tool.

The Wave+ has convinced me that a good multi-tool is more than merely an expensive gimmick. It may not replace dedicated tools, but having so many functions at my fingertips in a form that is so compact, portable and easy to use makes it a practical, worthwhile investment.

The Wave Plus is a multi-tool workhorse. It packs 18 tools in one, including sharp blades, spring scissors, wire cutters, and more.

US Treasury walks back crypto surveillance ‘travel rule’ proposal

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

FinCEN and the Federal Reserve Board of Governors rescinded the "Travel Rule," which was first proposed in 2020 to require financial institutions to collect information on funds worth $250 or more.

The Treasury Department has withdrawn a controversial proposal that would have expanded reporting requirements for cryptocurrency transactions.

On Friday, the Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network and the Federal Reserve Board of Governors rescinded the "Travel Rule," which was first proposed in 2020 to require financial institutions to collect information on transactions of $250 or more, decreasing that threshold from $3,000.

In a filing, FinCEN said it was withdrawing the proposed rule because more than five years had passed, the convertible virtual currency industry had changed and additional public input would be helpful.

"The Agencies believe that any future, potential amendments to the Recordkeeping Rule and Travel Rule related to CVC should be considered in light of these recent developments to ensure that such amendments are fit for purpose," FinCEN said.

When the rule was proposed, FinCEN had argued that bad actors were increasingly using smaller transactions to move illicit funds. However, crypto groups, like Coin Center, had warned that lowering the threshold from $3,000 to $250 would mean that more ordinary transactions would be subject to "warrantless financial surveillance."

In a blog on Friday, Coin Center said it was happy to see the rule proposal rescinded, along with another rule proposal that was withdrawn last month that would have imposed reporting and recordkeeping requirements on transactions involving self-hosted wallets.

"This follows Treasury’s withdrawal last month of the proposed 'unhosted wallet' and cryptocurrency mixing rules, both of which Coin Center had also opposed," the organization said. 'Taken together, these withdrawals close the books on several long-pending proposals that would have expanded the Bank Secrecy Act’s already extensive financial surveillance regime."

Crypto Council for Innovation CEO Ji Hun Kim said in a statement that withdrawing the travel rule was the "right call."

"It had not moved forward over the past several years for good reason," he said. "Lowering the threshold from $3,000 to $250 for cross-border transfers would have placed undue burden on institutions."

Binance hack is ‘cyber war,’ TRM Labs executive says

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

TRM Labs executive Ari Redbord called for governments to take offensive action against North Korean hackers following the record $6.3 billion Binance theft.

The $6.3 billion Binance hack sent shockwaves through the crypto industry. Following a series of increasingly severe cybersecurity incidents, the attack on the world’s biggest crypto exchange demonstrated how far state-backed hackers can, and will, go.

TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence company, was on the front lines of efforts to stop the theft, releasing a report in its aftermath. The firm was early to attribute the attack to the North Korean hacking group, TraitorTrader, citing “indisputable” onchain and offchain evidence. The $1.5 billion hack of Bybit in February 2025 was attributed to the same group.

Binance said in its postmortem on Nov. 3 that attackers posed as recruiters to plant malware on an engineer’s work computer, then used a compromised internal AI tool to reach its wallet-signing service.

Ari Redbord, TRM Labs’ global head of policy and government affairs, told Cointelegraph that although the industry’s coordinated response to the attack was unprecedented, “we are only going to see much more of this.”

The speed and scale of industry’s response to the hack were among the factors that stood out to Redbord.

Tether froze 971.1 million USDt (USDT) tokens just 37 minutes after the main sweep. Combined, stablecoin issuers froze tokens worth about $2.67 billion, according to TRM. “Nothing like that has ever been done before,” Redbord said. “They were able to claw back 40% right out the gate.”

The race to catch stolen funds before hackers can move them offchain is a critical part of defending against such attacks. TRM’s Redbord explained that the Beacon Network, an intelligence-sharing system TRM launched in 2025, allowed companies to coordinate quickly to immobilize the funds before working to seize them. “Normally I see a few Beacon alerts maybe every day. I’ve seen, I don’t know, hundreds or maybe even a thousand Beacon alerts over the last few days,” he said.

Related: Ethereum devs won’t fork Glamsterdam on Hoodi until smart contract risks resolved

The push for coordination comes as Bloomberg reported on Nov. 4 that the T3 Financial Crime Unit, which TRM formed with Tron and Tether in September 2024, disbanded in late August, with sources citing slow responses and poor coordination. Tether said it would continue working with TRM through Beacon.

Although Redbord called for greater coordination, he warned that such efforts may do little to deter future attacks. He noted that North Korea’s Oct. 29 theft was equivalent to roughly a third of the country’s GDP and accounted for half of the $12.5 billion stolen by state-backed hackers since 2017. Even if attackers recover only $1 billion, the potential payoff remains enormous, he argued.

“North Korea will keep doing this as long as it’s effective,” he said.

Redbord argued that stopping such attacks requires offensive action from governments in response to North Korea’s use of hackers operating as an arm of the state.

TRM’s capacity gap report found that the gap between attackers and cybersecurity defenders continues to widen, driven by hackers’ early adoption of frontier technologies such as AI. He said law enforcement and compliance teams need AI to find and patch vulnerabilities as quickly as hackers can exploit them.

“Every time North Korea hacks, we should be hacking them back,” he continued, calling for offensive cyber operations and action against laundering networks and banks that hackers use to cash out. “This is cyber war,” he said.

Please Do Not Buy Costco’s $520 Canon R100 Kit

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Please Do Not Buy Costco’s $520 Canon R100 Kit

The holiday season is ramping up and that once again means Canon is trotting out the same cash cow it has milked for the last three straight years. Costco (soon to be followed by other stores, surely) is offering a Canon EOS R100 kit for an enticing $520. Do not buy it.

Consider this a public service announcement, and a reiteration of the same plea I published two years ago (except, somehow, the R100 is more expensive now!). The only reason to buy the Canon EOS R100 is that it is cheap. Like, cheap cheap. It is easily the most affordable interchangeable lens mirrorless camera that you can buy brand-new right now, but that is quite literally the only thing it has going for it.

As an investment in someone’s future in photography, it is a terrible purchase.

“Using the R100 felt like going a few steps backward in camera technology. Yes, it’s simple in its intention and design, but maybe a little too simple,” Chris Niccolls wrote in his review. “Even significantly older cameras than the R100 outperform it. I don’t think I would recommend this camera to many people unless the budget had to remain absolutely fixed at sub-$500. Even then, I would probably just stick to my smartphone.”

We've been particularly harsh on what we call "toy cameras" lately, and those are basically any compact camera that repurposes what are glorified dashcam sensors and crams them into plastic camera-shaped bodies. Those are e-waste waiting to happen. The R100 is not e-waste in the sense that it is a camera that does take decent photos and features a removable battery. It is better than a piece of garbage, but the benchmark for a $500+ purchase should be significantly higher than "not garbage." There are better options out there that are within the same price tier.

While the R100 wins as the cheapest, there are three cameras that are better that can be purchased brand-new today for around the same price.

82 PixelMeter Nikon Z30 Reviewer Consensus: Strong The Nikon Z30 is a compact, user-friendly mirrorless camera designed primarily for vloggers and content creators who prioritize video production. While it excels at capturing sharp 4K video with reliable autofocus and a convenient vari-angle screen, it lacks an electronic viewfinder and in-body stabilization, making it less suitable for dedicated stills photography. Nikon Z30Buy new on B&HNikon Z30Buy used on KEH.com

First, let's consider the Nikon Z30. While it isn't perfect, it is a substantially better camera. While it does not have an electronic viewfinder, unlike the R100, it does feature a fully articulating rear touchscreen, is smaller and lighter than the R100, takes great video, and has excellent autofocus performance. It has a faster burst photo mode than the R100, has a good degree of ruggedness to it, and is overall a better feeling and operating camera. The Z30 is also substantially discounted versus its launch price: it costs under $600 now.

82 PixelMeter Canon EOS R50 Reviewer Consensus: Strong The Canon EOS R50 is a compact, entry-level mirrorless camera that offers impressive subject-tracking autofocus and sharp 4K video capabilities for beginners. While it serves as an excellent, user-friendly tool for content creators, its small form factor results in limited physical controls and a lack of in-body image stabilization. Canon EOS R50Buy new on B&HCanon EOS R50Buy used on KEH.com

Admittedly, the Z30 isn't the best photo-specific camera, so if that is more important to you when trying to find something better than the R100, then Canon's own R50 is just overall a better camera in every way. Everything that's wrong with the R100 is better on the R50: you get back an EVF, you get a fully articulating rear LCD, improved video features, and -- really importantly -- the R50 supports USB-C charging directly through the camera. One of the biggest drawbacks of the R100 is its lack of support for this, which makes traveling with the camera a hassle since you always have to carry the wall charger with you. The Canon R50 is available today for $679.

84 PixelMeter Canon EOS R50 V Reviewer Consensus: Strong The Canon EOS R50 V is a compact, video-centric mirrorless camera that provides creators with high-quality 4K output, 10-bit color, and convenient vertical shooting tools. Although it lacks in-body image stabilization and an electronic viewfinder, its reliable autofocus and accessible controls make it a strong entry point for those upgrading from smartphones. Canon EOS R50 VBuy new on B&HCanon EOS R50 VBuy used on KEH.com

The R50V, which is a video-focused version of the R50, is also a great choice if you want to stick with Canon but won't miss a viewfinder and want to get even closer to the R100's price point. The R50V is basically the R50 with its EVF chopped off, and for that, it's available for $569.

I will admit, it is impossible to buy any other mirrorless camera brand-new that comes with a lens and memory card for $520. The lens Canon bundles with the R100 isn't good, but to get it separately costs $350. That means the R100 actually is worth just $170 as part of this kit. Again, this camera's only selling point is that it is cheap as dirt. But I beg you to find something that's affordable and also good.

Right now at KEH, you can get a Canon 5D Mark II in excellent condition for $296. You can then pick up a 24-105mm f/4L for $475 or the more entry-level option of 24-105mm f/3.5-5.6 for $256. Both options are slightly more expensive than Costco's kit, but are substantially better combos that will actually provide a burgeoning photographer with the ability to learn and grow. There are so many other possibilities on the used market, too. This is just one example.

Canon 5D Mark IIBuy used on KEH.comCanon EF 24-105mm f/4 L IS USMBuy used on KEH.comCanon EF 24-105mm f/3.5-5.6 IS STMBuy used on KEH.com

This all takes me back to my real main issue with the R100. It's barely fine as far as technology goes, but it is so limited that it serves as a poor camera to invest anything more than a first glance into the world of photography with. It cannot grow with someone beyond the very first stage of photography and after that, they're going to want one of these other cameras I've mentioned that costs just a little bit more. Why spend that amount twice? Skip the R100 stage and go for a camera that can support several years of interest, not just a few weeks.

I love photography. It would break my heart if the reason someone didn't realize they loved it too was because the experience of the R100 was so bland and uninteresting that they gave up on the hobby before it had time to take root.
